#### **EXECUTIVE SUMMARY**

#### **Coral Communities**

The coral reefs of the five main volcanic islands of American Samoa have experienced a series of large scale disturbances over the last few decades.

--- SENTENCE 14 ---
The effects of these disturbances have been most severe on the main island of Tutuila and nearby Aunu'u.

--- SENTENCE 15 ---
In the late 1970s, the lush coral communities on Tutuila and Aunu'u were devastated by a major COTS outbreak.

--- SENTENCE 16 ---
Recovery was well underway by the early 1990s, when the reefs were devastated again by two severe hurricanes.

--- SENTENCE 17 ---
By the mid 1990s, recovery was underway again, despite a mass coral bleaching event in 1994.

--- SENTENCE 18 ---
Most of the reefs on Tutuila and Aunu'u have continued to show a rapid recovery over the last few years, and now comprise lush coral communities.

--- SENTENCE 19 ---
The reefs on Aunu'u (see below) and the north side of Tutuila (eg Vatia), are in particularly good condition and are quite spectacular.

--- SENTENCE 20 ---
These results demonstrate that most of the reefs on these islands are healthy and resilient to large scale disturbances.

!

--- SENTENCE 21 ---
Coral communities at Aunu'u (photos: L. Basch, NPAS)

Unfortunately, some of the reefs on Tutuila are not in good condition, probably due to poor water quality.

--- SENTENCE 22 ---
For example, sites that receive high sediment loads (eg Fagasa, Fagafue, Faga'alu), tend to have lower coral cover than elsewhere around the island, and comprise distinctive coral communities dominated by species that can tolerate high sediment loads (eg *Porites* and *Diploastrea*: see below).

--- SENTENCE 23 ---
Even the reefs in Pago Pago Harbour are showing signs of improvement, probably due to improved water quality.

--- SENTENCE 24 ---
In particular, good coral recruitment has been recorded at some sites for the first time in decades.

--- SENTENCE 25 ---
This includes species that are particularly sensitive to poor water quality (eg *Acropora* species), which have been absent or rare in the Harbour since the 1950s.

!

--- SENTENCE 26 ---
Coral community at Fagasa dominated by *Porites* colonies (left), and large *Diploastrea* colony (12m diam.)

--- SENTENCE 27 ---
at Faga'alu (photos: L. Basch, NPAS)

Despite recent improvements, there are still problems with water quality in the Harbour (eg chronic fuel spills), and the reefs remain in the worst condition of all the reefs in the Territory.

--- SENTENCE 28 ---
Unfortunately, the lush coral communities described in the Harbour early last century have not been seen for decades.

--- SENTENCE 29 ---
Despite these problems, the reefs in the Harbour are quite important, because they support habitats and species otherwise unique to Samoa.

--- SENTENCE 30 ---
A good example is the coral community at Faga'alu, which is dominated by large massive and foliaceus colonies of *Diploastrea*, *Oxypora*, and *Merulina* and *Lobophyllia* (see below).

!

--- SENTENCE 31 ---
[](_page_4_Picture_4.jpeg)

Distinctive coral community at Faga'alu (photos: L. Basch, NPAS)

--- SENTENCE 32 ---
A different pattern is apparent in the Manu'a Islands.

--- SENTENCE 33 ---
These reefs were devastated by Hurricane Tusi in 1987, but escaped damage from the major COTS outbreak in the late 1970s and the most recent hurricanes.

--- SENTENCE 34 ---
By the mid 1990s, they had largely recovered from the effects of Hurricane Tusi, and most were in good condition.

--- SENTENCE 35 ---
Unfortunately, there has been a decline in the coral communities on the reef slope on Ofu and Olosega over the last few years, probably due chronic COTS predation.

--- SENTENCE 36 ---
In contrast, the coral communities in Ofu Lagoon have not declined, and remain in good condition.

--- SENTENCE 37 ---
However these reefs are dominated by large *Porites* and *Millepora* colonies (see below), which are characteristic of remanent coral communities after COTS predation.

--- SENTENCE 38 ---
These communities remain among the most spectacular in the Territory.

!

--- SENTENCE 39 ---
Coral communities in Ofu Lagoon (photos: L. Basch, NPAS)

--- SENTENCE 40 ---
The reefs of Tau are in good condition, and coral cover has increased over the last few years.

--- SENTENCE 41 ---
Some of these reefs are particularly important, because they support some of the largest coral colonies recorded in Samoa (see below).

--- SENTENCE 42 ---
These colonies are rare, have high conservation value, and should be protected.

!

--- SENTENCE 43 ---
[](_page_5_Picture_3.jpeg)

Very large *Porites* bommie (10m diam.)

--- SENTENCE 44 ---
at Afuli Cove, Tau (photos: L. Basch, NPAS).

#### Mass Coral Bleaching

In early 2002 (Jan to March), American Samoa was on the edge of a widespread temperature anomaly in the Pacific Ocean (see below).

--- SENTENCE 45 ---
Temperatures were recorded up to 2o C above normal in some locations (eg the Great Barrier Reef), which caused severe coral bleaching.

--- SENTENCE 46 ---
The reefs of Samoa experienced sea temperatures close to the threshold where bleaching was likely to occur (0.5-0.75o C).

--- SENTENCE 47 ---
This study confirmed that the reefs on the five main volcanic islands experienced low to moderate bleaching in March 2002 (see below), with the highest levels of bleaching recorded on the north side of Tutuila.

--- SENTENCE 48 ---
Bleaching was less severe than in 1994, which remains the worst coral bleaching event on record in American Samoa.

!

--- SENTENCE 49 ---
[](_page_6_Picture_4.jpeg)

Sea water temperature anomaly (top: NOAA 2002) and coral bleaching in Ofu Lagoon (bottom: L. Basch, NPAS)

#### **Reef Fish Communities**

Coral communities provide important habitat for reef fishes, and there have been some major changes in the fish communities on Tutuila and Aunu'u over the last few decades, in response to changes in both the coral communities (see above) and human activities (particularly fishing).

--- SENTENCE 50 ---
In the mid 1970s, the reefs of Tutuila and Aunu'u supported a rich and diverse fish fauna, because the reefs were in good condition and fishing pressure was relatively low.

--- SENTENCE 51 ---
When the reefs were devastated by COTS in the late 1970s, there were major impacts on some components of the fish fauna.

--- SENTENCE 52 ---
In particular, there was a decline in abundance of species that are closely associated with the coral communities.

--- SENTENCE 53 ---
Two good examples are the damselfish *Plectroglyphidodon dickii* and butterflyfish *Chaetodon trifascialis* (see below), which are closely associated with branching and plate coral.

--- SENTENCE 54 ---
The populations of these species have started to recover in the last few years, along with their host corals.

--- SENTENCE 55 ---
Impacts on other fish species by the COTS outbreak, including fisheries species, were surprisingly small.

!

--- SENTENCE 56 ---
[](_page_7_Picture_3.jpeg)

*Plectroglyphidodon dickii* (left) and *Chaetodon trifascialis* (photos: L. Basch, NPAS).

--- SENTENCE 57 ---
While some components of the fish fauna now appear to be in good condition on Tutuila and Aunu'u, others are conspicuous by their absence (or small size and low abundance).

--- SENTENCE 58 ---
This is due to the impacts of fishing on the major fisheries families (particularly groupers, parrotfishes, and snappers).

--- SENTENCE 59 ---
When the fish communities are compared among islands that have recently experienced low, moderate and high levels of fishing (Manu'a Islands, Aunu'u and Tutuila respectively), it is clear that the fish populations on Tutuila are overfished.

--- SENTENCE 60 ---
Fisheries species are much less abundant on Tutuila and Aunu'u than in the Manu'a Islands.

--- SENTENCE 61 ---
Furthermore, large species that are particularly vulnerable to overfishing (sharks, some parrotfishes and maori wrasse: see below) are now rare or absent on Tutuila and Aunu'u, but still occur in the Manu'a Islands.

--- SENTENCE 62 ---
Some of these species, particularly parrotfishes, were heavily targeted by the commercial nightime scuba fishery that operated on Tutuila from 1995 to 2001.

!

--- SENTENCE 63 ---
Maori wrasse (photo: R. Myers); and blacktip reef shark, Ofu (photo: L. Basch, NPAS)

--- SENTENCE 64 ---
These results demonstrate that the Governor made the right decision to ban the scuba fishery.

--- SENTENCE 65 ---
If fishing pressure can be maintained at low levels on Tutuila over the next few years, the fish communities may recover from the effects of fishing, since these species still occur in the Territory (particularly in the Manu'a Islands).

--- SENTENCE 66 ---
However, it may be several years before the first signs of recovery are apparent.

--- SENTENCE 67 ---
The fish communities on Ofu and Olosega have been affected by the impacts of chronic COTS predation on the coral communities on those islands.

--- SENTENCE 68 ---
In particular, species that are dependant corals that are the preferred food of the starfish (eg branching or plate coral: see above) are uncommon.

#### Mass Recruitment of Surgeonfish (*pala'ia*)

--- SENTENCE 69 ---
In March 2002, the reefs of American Samoa experienced a mass recruitment event of one of the major fisheries species, *Ctenochaetus striatus*.

--- SENTENCE 70 ---
In some places, the recruits (locally known as *pala'ia* ) were present in very high densities and formed large schools (up to 5000 individuals), which roved over the reef flat, lagoon and outer reef slope (see below).

!

--- SENTENCE 71 ---
[](_page_8_Picture_6.jpeg)

*Pala'ia* schools in the lagoon and on the reef slope at Ofu (photos: P. Craig & L. Basch, NPAS).

--- SENTENCE 72 ---
Mass recruitment of this species appears to be a fairly predictable event in American Samoa, which occurs around the new moon in February/March each year.

--- SENTENCE 73 ---
These events are well known to the Samoan people, who target them in a specific, tailor made fishery.

--- SENTENCE 74 ---
This is somewhat analogous to the way in which Samoans predict and utilise the spawning of the palolo worm, which is also available to the fishery for only a few days each year (and is related to the same lunar phase in October and/or November).

--- SENTENCE 75 ---
*Pala'ia* were also targeted by carnivorous fishes (eg jacks), which were observed striking at the schools.

--- SENTENCE 76 ---
Not surprisingly, mortality was high.

--- SENTENCE 77 ---
Further studies are required to understand the population dynamics of this important species in Samoa.

#### **Key Macroinvertebrates**

#### Giant Clams

In a similar pattern to the fish, giant clams (see below) were more abundant in the Manu'a Islands (particularly on Tau) than on Tutuila and Aunu'u.

--- SENTENCE 78 ---
Given that giant clams are highly prized in the fishery, this is probably due to overfishing on Tutuila and nearby Aunu'u.

--- SENTENCE 79 ---
One concern is that the remaining individuals on most islands in American Samoa (particularly Tutuila) are now present in such low densities that their reproductive success and subsequent recruitment may be limited.

--- SENTENCE 80 ---
Indeed giant clam recruitment is low on most of the main volcanic islands, except Tau.

--- SENTENCE 81 ---
These results confirm the importance of Rose Atoll as a refuge for giant clams in American Samoa, and highlights the importance of Tau as a potential refuge for giant clams in the main volcanic islands.

--- SENTENCE 82 ---
#### Crown-of-thorns Starfish

There was a major crown-of-thorns starfish (COTS) outbreak on Tutuila and Aunu'u in the late 1970s, which devastated the coral communities.

--- SENTENCE 83 ---
COTS were rare on those islands for several decades prior to that event, and have been rare ever since.

--- SENTENCE 84 ---
However, Samoan traditional knowledge indicates that starfish outbreaks have occurred on Tutuila in the past.

--- SENTENCE 85 ---
In contrast, the reefs on Ofu and Olosega in the Manu'a Group appear to support chronic low to moderate populations of the starfish (see below), which have played an important role in structuring the coral reef communities on the those islands.

--- SENTENCE 86 ---
One hypothesis is that the ongoing presence of the starfish may be related to the presence of the lagoon on Ofu.

--- SENTENCE 87 ---
A similar situation may exist on 'Upolu in neighbouring Samoa.

!

--- SENTENCE 88 ---
[](_page_9_Picture_8.jpeg)

Giant clam and crown-of-thorns starfish, Ofu Lagoon (photos: L. Basch, NPAS)

#### **Marine Protected Areas**

Marine Protected Areas (MPAs) can play an important role in protecting biodiversity, and as a fisheries management tool.

--- SENTENCE 89 ---
At present, only 6% of reefs in American Samoa are MPAs, which is much less than the 20-50% recommended by scientists.

--- SENTENCE 90 ---
More 'no-take' MPAs should be established in American Samoa, particularly on Tutuila (or nearby Aunu'u), were overfishing is a problem.

--- SENTENCE 91 ---
The best candidates for new MPAs in American Samoa include Aunu'u, Vatia (Tutuila), Afuli Cove (Tau), Asaga (Ofu) and Sili (Olosega).

--- SENTENCE 92 ---
This survey included sites in three of the four existing MPAs in American Samoa: Fagatele Bay National Marine Sanctuary (FBNMS), the Ofu Unit of the National Park of American Samoa (NPAS), and the Ofu-Vaoto Marine Park.

--- SENTENCE 93 ---
Therefore, it provides an opportunity to assess the status of the reefs in these MPAs, and compare them to other reefs in the Territory.

--- SENTENCE 94 ---
The coral reefs of FBNMS (see below) have recovered well from the large scale disturbances of the last few decades, and are now in good condition.

--- SENTENCE 95 ---
In fact the reefs in the Sanctuary support some of the healthiest coral communities on Tutuila.

--- SENTENCE 96 ---
Unfortunately, like most other places on the island, Fagatele Bay appears to have been overfished.

--- SENTENCE 97 ---
The density and biomass of the major fisheries families are relatively low, and several large reef fish species that are particularly vulnerable to overfishing are now rare or absent.

--- SENTENCE 98 ---
This highlights the need for improved enforcement of the fishing restrictions in the Bay.

--- SENTENCE 99 ---
The reefs in the Ofu Unit of the NPAS (see below) are also in good condition.

--- SENTENCE 100 ---
The NPAS includes Ofu Lagoon, which is the best developed natural lagoon system on the main volcanic islands.

--- SENTENCE 101 ---
Despite chronic COTS predation, the lagoon supports spectacular coral reef communities (see above), which are otherwise unique in American Samoa.

--- SENTENCE 102 ---
The lagoon may also play an important role in the ecology of the reefs on Ofu and Olosega, since it may act as a nursery for some important fisheries species (particularly parrotfishes), and play an important role in maintaining the chronic COTS population on those islands (see above).

--- SENTENCE 103 ---
The Ofu-Vaoto National Park is part of the same lagoonal system as the NPAS, and requires protection.

--- SENTENCE 104 ---
However, the coral reef communities are not as spectacular, because the large massive corals that dominate the lagoon in the NPAS are less abundant.

!

--- SENTENCE 105 ---
[](_page_10_Picture_6.jpeg)

National Park of American Samoa, Ofu Unit (photo P. Craig) and Fagatele Bay National Marine Sanctuary (photo FBNMS).

#### **Long Term Monitoring**

This study demonstrates the important role that long term monitoring programs can play in understanding the natural variability and long term trends in the coral reefs of American Samoa.

--- SENTENCE 106 ---
One benefit of this study is that it provides an overview of the condition of the reefs on all the main volcanic islands simultaneously.

--- SENTENCE 107 ---
It also provides a broad scale perspective for understanding the results of the site dedicated monitoring programs in Fagatele Bay National Marine Sanctuary and Pago Pago Harbour (Aua transect: see below), which provide a much longer term perspective on the reefs of Tutuila (85 and 25 years respectively).

!

--- SENTENCE 108 ---
Aua Transect in Pago Pago Harbour (on reef flat in foreground) in 1917 (photo: Mayor 1924a) and 1996 (photo: A. Green).

--- SENTENCE 109 ---
Unfortunately, the two remote atolls (Rose and Swains) could not be resurveyed this year, due to logistic constraints.

--- SENTENCE 110 ---
They should be resurveyed as soon as possible, particularly Rose, due to the high conservation status of the atoll.

--- SENTENCE 111 ---
## **INTRODUCTION**

Coral reefs are diverse marine ecosystems that flourish in the clear, tropical waters of the South Pacific.

--- SENTENCE 112 ---
American Samoa is fortunate to have well developed coral reefs surrounding all islands in the Territory (Green 1996a).

--- SENTENCE 113 ---
These reefs are an important natural resource for the Samoan people, since they provide the basis for the valuable inshore fishery (Craig et al 1993, Craig 2002).

--- SENTENCE 114 ---
They also play an integral role in the rich cultural heritage of the islands, and provide other important ecosystem services (including shoreline protection).

--- SENTENCE 115 ---
## **Large Scale Disturbances**

Unfortunately, the reefs of American Samoa have experienced a series of large scale disturbances over the last few decades, including a major outbreak of the coralivorous crown-of-thorns starfish (*Acanthaster planci*), several severe hurricanes, and mass coral bleaching events (Green 1996a, Green et al 1999).

--- SENTENCE 116 ---
The reefs on the main island of Tutuila and nearby Aunu'u have been devastated by these disturbances on several occasions, while those in the Manu'a Islands and two remote atolls have escaped serious damage from most of these events (Green 1996a).

--- SENTENCE 117 ---
The crown-of-thorns starfish (COTS, locally know as *alamea*) is a natural inhabitant of the reefs of Samoa.

--- SENTENCE 118 ---
This species feeds on corals and is usually uncommon, where it causes minimal damage to coral communities.

--- SENTENCE 119 ---
However, this species is subject to dramatic increases in numbers, called population outbreaks.

--- SENTENCE 120 ---
The degree to which these outbreaks are caused by natural or human related activities remains a matter for debate.

--- SENTENCE 121 ---
Whatever the cause, starfish outbreaks can cause major damage to coral reefs.

--- SENTENCE 122 ---
Even moderate outbreaks have been know to cause major damage over a period of several years (Zann 1992).

--- SENTENCE 123 ---
In 1977-79, the reefs of Tutuila experienced a major COTS outbreak (Birkeland & Randall 1979, Birkeland et al 1987).

--- SENTENCE 124 ---
This was an unusual event for Tutuila, since starfish were rare on the island for several decades prior to this event (Birkeland & Randall1979, Birkeland 1982, Birkeland & Lucas 1990).

--- SENTENCE 125 ---
However, Samoan traditional knowledge indicates that starfish outbreaks may have been a recurring phenomenon in the past (Birkeland & Randall 1979, Birkeland 1981, Birkeland & Lucas 1990, Zann 1992).

--- SENTENCE 126 ---
Birkeland and co-workers proposed that the outbreak was due to heavy rainfall following a period of drought, which washed a pulse of nutrients into the water (Birkeland & Randall 1979, Birkeland 1982).

--- SENTENCE 127 ---
This increase in nutrients may have increased the survival of starfish during their planktonic larval stage, by stimulating phytoplankton blooms which provide food for the larvae.

--- SENTENCE 128 ---
This major outbreak in the late 1970s caused severe damage to the coral communities around most of Tutuila and Aunu'u (Birkeland & Randall 1979, Birkeland et al 1987, Zann 1992), although some bays escaped damage (Birkeland et al 1987, Green et al 1997a).

--- SENTENCE 129 ---
Even though large scale control measures were undertaken (~487,000 were removed: Birkeland 1982, Zann 1992), the starfish remained abundant and systematically devastated the coral communities on these islands (Birkeland & Randall 1979).

--- SENTENCE 130 ---
The impacts of the COTS outbreak are well described based on the long term monitoring program in Fagatele Bay National Marine Sanctuary (FBNMS: Green et al 1999).

--- SENTENCE 131 ---
Prior to the starfish outbreak, the reefs in Fagatele Bay comprised healthy coral reef communities characterised by high coral cover (30-50%: especially table *Acropora*).

--- SENTENCE 132 ---
Unfortunately, the coral communities in Fagatele Bay were devastated by the starfish outbreak in 1979, which lead to a dramatic reduction in coral cover in the Bay.

--- SENTENCE 133 ---
The effects of the starfish outbreak tended to be most severe in more sheltered locations (i.e. deeper water >9m), and less severe in more exposed locations (i.e. shallow water < 6m).

--- SENTENCE 134 ---
It was assumed that this was because the starfish were unable to maintain their position on the substrate in areas of strong surge.

--- SENTENCE 135 ---
Almost 10 years later, the coral communities in deeper water had started to recover from the starfish outbreak.

--- SENTENCE 136 ---
Coral communities provide important habitat for coral reef fishes, and long term monitoring of Fagatele Bay and other sites around Tutuila, showed that there were some changes in the fish communities as a result of the habitat degradation caused by the starfish outbreak (Birkeland et al 1987, 1996, *in prep*, Buckley 1986, Green

--- SENTENCE 137 ---
In particular, there was a dramatic decline in small, site-attached species that are closely associated with live coral colonies (such as the damselfish *Plectroglyphidodon dickii* and the hawkfish *Paracirrhites arcatus*), and an increase in species that prefer coral rubble or algae.

--- SENTENCE 138 ---
Impacts on other species, including fisheries species, were surprisingly small (Buckley 1986, Birkeland et al. 1987).

--- SENTENCE 139 ---
Fortunately, the reefs of the Manu'a Islands were not affected by the massive starfish outbreak that devastated the reefs on Tutuila in the late 1970s (D. Itano *pers.

--- SENTENCE 140 ---
However, COTS predation appears to have been chronic (at low to moderate levels) in the Manu'a Islands for many years (particularly on Ofu), which has probably caused some damage to the reefs (Itano & Buckley 1988a, Zann 1992, Green 1996a, Mundy 1996).

--- SENTENCE 141 ---
In particular, COTS predation has probably had a significant impact on the coral communities in Ofu Lagoon, by favouring less preferred prey species (particularly massive *Porites* and *Millepora*) and disadvantaging preferred species (particularly *Acropora* :

--- SENTENCE 142 ---
The reefs on the main island of 'Upolu in neighbouring Samoa, also appear to experience chronic low to moderate levels of COTS predation (Zann 1991, 1992, Green 1996a,b), as well as occasional large scale outbreaks (including the late 1970s at the same time as the outbreak on Tutuila:

--- SENTENCE 143 ---
Birkeland & Randall 1979.

--- SENTENCE 144 ---
In contrast, very few COTS have been observed on the two remote atolls (Rose and Swains).

--- SENTENCE 145 ---
This is consistent with Birkeland's hypothesis, that COTS outbreaks tend to occur around high islands and not around atolls (Birkeland 1982).

--- SENTENCE 146 ---
#### Hurricanes

The reefs of American Samoa are subject to infrequent but sometimes severe hurricanes.

--- SENTENCE 147 ---
In the early 1990s, the reefs of Tutuila and Aunu'u experienced two severe hurricanes (Ofa in 1990 and Val in 1991:

--- SENTENCE 148 ---
Green et al. 1999), which caused major damage to the reefs (especially Val: Birkeland et al 1996, Green 1996a, Green et al 1999).

--- SENTENCE 149 ---
The impacts of these hurricanes on Tutuila are well described based on the long term monitoring of FBNMS (Green et al 1999).

--- SENTENCE 150 ---
This study showed that in contrast to the COTS outbreak (see above), the hurricanes affected the coral communities in shallower, inner portions of the bay (<9m) to the greatest extent.

--- SENTENCE 151 ---
They also caused major changes to the physical structure of the reef, since large coral colonies were overturned and destroyed.

--- SENTENCE 152 ---
Fortunately, the reefs of Fagatele Bay have proved resilient to such disturbances, and recovery from the hurricanes was already well underway by the mid to late 1990s (Green et al 1999).

--- SENTENCE 153 ---
Reef slopes had been consolidated with a lush growth of pink coralline algae, and coral recruitment was high.

--- SENTENCE 154 ---
Most other sites around Tutuila and Aunu'u were also in the early stages of recovery by the mid 1990s (Green 1996a, Mundy 1996), particularly where water quality was good (Green 1996a).

--- SENTENCE 155 ---
The Manu'a Group and Rose Atoll were less affected by the most recent hurricanes, but were badly hit by Hurricane Tusi in 1987 (P. Craig *pers.

--- SENTENCE 156 ---
Swains Island experienced a violent storm that devastated the island and reefs in 1987 (Green 1996c).

--- SENTENCE 157 ---
However, Green (1996a,c) showed that the reefs on these islands had recovered from these disturbances, and were in good to excellent condition again by the mid-1990s.

--- SENTENCE 158 ---
In 1998, Hurricane Ron passed within 8km northeast of Swains Island, although it did not appear to cause significant damage to the reefs on the atoll (Page & Green 1998).

--- SENTENCE 159 ---
Hurricane Ofa also caused major damage to the coral reefs on the neighbouring island of 'Upolu in Samoa (Zann & Sua 1991), although recovery was well underway by the mid 1990s (Green 1996b).

#### Mass Coral Bleaching

Coral bleaching is a stress condition in corals which involves a breakdown of the symbiotic relationship between corals and unicellular algae (zooxanthellae: GBRMPA 2002).

--- SENTENCE 160 ---
These microscopic plants live within the coral tissue and provide the coral with food and their normal healthy colour.

--- SENTENCE 161 ---
The symptoms of bleaching include a loss of colour as zooxanthellae are expelled from the coral tissue, sometimes leaving corals bone white.

--- SENTENCE 162 ---
Bleaching stress is also exhibited by other reef animals that have a symbiotic relationship with zooxanthellae, such as soft corals, giant clams, and some sponges.

--- SENTENCE 163 ---
While many different stresses can cause coral bleaching, the main cause of widespread bleaching is elevated sea temperature (GBRMPA 2002).

--- SENTENCE 164 ---
Additional stresses such as high light intensity, low salinity and pollutants are known to exacerbate these effects.

--- SENTENCE 165 ---
Reef corals are very sensitive to sea temperatures outside their normal range.

--- SENTENCE 166 ---
Elevated temperatures of 1o C above the long term monthly summer average are enough to cause coral bleaching in many dominant coral species (GBRMPA 2002).

--- SENTENCE 167 ---
If conditions are only mildly stressful, corals can recover from bleaching, but if conditions are severe enough, they may die.

--- SENTENCE 168 ---
In early 1994, American Samoa experienced unusually hot and still weather conditions, which resulted in unusually high water temperatures (N. Daschbach *pers comm*) and stressful conditions for corals.

--- SENTENCE 169 ---
These conditions resulted in the most serious coral bleaching event ever recorded in Samoa.

--- SENTENCE 170 ---
During this event, coral bleaching was severe and widespread (at least Tutuila and Manu'a Islands: Craig et al. 1995, Goreau & Hayes 1994) and extended down to a depth of 30m in some places (eg Masefau and FBNMS).

--- SENTENCE 171 ---
Observations from FBNMS indicate that bleaching was most pronounced in the shallow, inner portions of the Bay (N. Daschbach *pers comm*).

--- SENTENCE 172 ---
Bleaching affected several taxa including hard corals, anemones and zooanthids, and some families of hard coral were more severely affected than others (especially Pocilloporidae: N. Daschbach *pers comm*, Birkeland et al 1996).

--- SENTENCE 173 ---
The impact of this event on the reefs of American Samoa is unclear, since it is unknown how much of the coral and other benthos recovered or died.

--- SENTENCE 174 ---
However, coral mortality was estimated to be high in some locations (eg 50% in Ofu Lagoon: P. Craig *pers comm*).

--- SENTENCE 175 ---
In March 1998, American Samoa again experienced unusually hot and still weather conditions accompanied by unusually low tides.

--- SENTENCE 176 ---
This resulted in widespread death of corals on the reef flat and crest at many sites around Tutuila (Birkeland et al *in prep*), and elsewhere in the archipelago (eg 'Upolu).

--- SENTENCE 177 ---
In early 2002 (January to March), a temperature anomaly with sea surface temperatures up to 1.5-2o C higher than long term seasonal averages was detected in the Western Pacific (NOAA 2002a), with the worst affected area centered on the Great Barrier Reef in Australia.

--- SENTENCE 178 ---
As a result, the GBR experienced its worse coral bleaching event on record (GBRMPA 2002).

--- SENTENCE 179 ---
The warm water anomaly that caused the bleaching on the GBR extended east across the Pacific to Fiji, which also experienced bleaching (ReefBase 2002).

--- SENTENCE 180 ---
American Samoa was right on the edge of this hot spot, and experienced sea surface temperatures 0.5-0.75o C above normal (NOAA 2002a).

--- SENTENCE 181 ---
This indicated that sea surface temperatures in American Samoa may have reached levels where bleaching was likely to occur.

## **Human Impacts**

In the absence of serious human impacts, coral reefs are resilient natural ecosystems that can recover from most large scale disturbances in one to two decades.

--- SENTENCE 182 ---
This is the case for most of the reefs in American Samoa (Green 1996a, Green et al 1999).

--- SENTENCE 183 ---
However, there is some concern regarding human impacts in some locations, particularly on the heavily populated island of Tutuila (especially in Pago Pago Harbour).

--- SENTENCE 184 ---
In some situations, human activities may have caused a decline in coral reef health, which has inhibited their ability to recover from large scale disturbances (Green 1996a).

--- SENTENCE 185 ---
Of particular concern are impacts from overfishing and poor water quality (Craig 2002).

--- SENTENCE 186 ---
Fortunately, human impacts appear to be less of a threat to the reefs on the less populated islands of the Manu'a Group, and minor on the two remote atolls (except for the shipwreck on Rose: Green 1996a, Green et al 1997b).

--- SENTENCE 187 ---
Aunu'u may experience moderate levels of human impacts due to the relatively high population density of this small island (see *Description of Study Area*), and its proximity to the main island of Tutuila.

#### Fishing

Like most Pacific Island countries, American Samoa has undergone many social, economic and environmental changes last century.

--- SENTENCE 188 ---
For example, there has been a shift from a subsistence to a mixed economy, which now includes both market and subsistence sectors (Hill 1977, Craig et al 1993).

--- SENTENCE 189 ---
Where once families depended on the coral reefs and plantations for their livelihood, many now receive monetary income from working for the government or industry.

--- SENTENCE 190 ---
This has been accompanied by a change in the nature of the local fishery from a subsistence level to a largely artisanal and recreational fishery, with some subsistence fishing continuing (Hill 1977, Craig et al 1993).

--- SENTENCE 191 ---
In addition, fishing practises have shifted from the use of traditional methods including *paopao* canoes and specialised fishing methods (eg fish traps, nets and lures), to modern methods including the use of power boats, scuba equipment and spearguns (Wass 1980).

--- SENTENCE 192 ---
There has also been a decline in traditional fisheries management practices (Wass 1980).

--- SENTENCE 193 ---
Accompanying these changes, has been a massive increase in the human population.

--- SENTENCE 194 ---
On the main island of Tutuila, the population has increased dramatically from about 5,000 in 1900 to the present level of 55,400 in 2000 (American Samoa Census 2000).

--- SENTENCE 195 ---
Most of the population live on Tutuila (96.7%), with a much smaller percentage on Aunu'u (0.8%), the Manu'a Islands (2.4%) and Swains Island (<0.1%).

--- SENTENCE 196 ---
The population is continuing to increase at a very fast rate, with a 22% increase recorded between 1990 and 2000.

--- SENTENCE 197 ---
This was primarily due to an increase in the population on Tutuila, since the population in Manu'a has declined in recent years (see *Description of Study Area*).

--- SENTENCE 198 ---
Limited information is available for the coral reef fishery in the Territory, and most of that which is available is for the main island of Tutuila (Craig et al 1993, Craig 2002).

--- SENTENCE 199 ---
Coral reef resources are harvested on a daily basis on Tutuila, and comprise 40-80% of the fisheries landings each year (Craig et al 1993, Saucerman 1995, 1996).

--- SENTENCE 200 ---
A monitoring program of the coral reef fishery in and around Pago Pago Harbour, detected a decline in subsistence catch and catch per unit effort from 1979 to 1991- 1995 (Saucerman 1995, 1996).

--- SENTENCE 201 ---
Saucerman (1995) concluded that while these were warning signs for the fishery, there did not appear to be a significant problem with overfishing at that time.

--- SENTENCE 202 ---
Unfortunately, this monitoring program was discontinued from 1995-2001, but was recommenced this year.

--- SENTENCE 203 ---
The artisanal catch was also monitored in 1994, but is currently assessed using market invoices with limited success (Craig 2002).

--- SENTENCE 204 ---
In the mid 1990s, a new, high technology commercial fishery became established on Tutuila (the nightime scuba fishery: Page 1998).

--- SENTENCE 205 ---
This type of fishery can quickly lead to overfishing, because the fish are particularly vulnerable to capture while sleeping at night.

--- SENTENCE 206 ---
The use of scuba exacerbates the situation, because the fishermen are able to dive deeper for longer, and are able to catch fish that were previously afforded some protection in deeper water.

--- SENTENCE 207 ---
The nightime scuba fishery led to a dramatic increase in the catch of reef fishes on the island.

--- SENTENCE 208 ---
Page (1998) demonstrated that parrotfishes were heavily exploited by this fishery, with a 15 fold increase in catch while it was operating.

--- SENTENCE 209 ---
He also estimated that 18.7% of the standing crop of parrotfishes on Tutuila was harvested in just one year (1997).

--- SENTENCE 210 ---
One concern was that many parrotfishes were being caught before they reached sexual maturity, which could lead to a reduction in the number of young fish recruiting to the reef in future (Page 1998).

--- SENTENCE 211 ---
This highly efficient fishery was banned by Executive Order by the Governor of American Samoa in April 2001 (and subsequently banned by DMWR regulation in January 2002), due to concerns that the reef fish populations were being overfished.

--- SENTENCE 212 ---
Fortunately, the fishery did not become established on the other islands in the Territory.

--- SENTENCE 213 ---
A case study of the response to the nightime scuba fishery is provided in Attachment 1.

Fishing rates are largely unknown for the other islands.

--- SENTENCE 214 ---
Despite the paucity of information, fishing pressure is presumed to be lower in the lightly populated Manu'a Islands.

--- SENTENCE 215 ---
For example, Itano & Buckley (1988a) reported that the Manu'a Islands appeared to be lightly fished, based on the presence of large, unwary fish and high densities of giant clams.

--- SENTENCE 216 ---
Fortunately, the NPAS has recently commenced a survey of the coral reef fisheries in the Manu'a Islands, which will provide the first quantitative fisheries data for those islands (P. Craig *pers comm*).

--- SENTENCE 217 ---
In contrast, fishing pressure on Aunu'u is presumed to be moderate, based on the relatively high population density on this small island (see *Description of Study Area*), and its close proximity to Tutuila.

--- SENTENCE 218 ---
Fishing pressure on the remote atolls is presumed to be light on Swains (which has a small population), and limited to isolated instances on Rose (which is uninhabited).

--- SENTENCE 219 ---
The major components of the coral reef fishery in American Samoa are reef fish, giant clams and the palolo worm (Ponwith 1991, Craig et al 1993).

--- SENTENCE 220 ---
At present, the most important reef fish families caught are surgeonfishes, groupers, snappers, parrotfishes and squirrelfishes (Saucerman 1995, Craig et al 1997).

--- SENTENCE 221 ---
Archaeological studies in the Manu'a Islands indicate that reef fish (and these families in particular) and giant clams have been important components of the fishery for thousands of years (Nagaoka 1993).

--- SENTENCE 222 ---
Several studies have examined the effects of fishing on fisheries resources in American Samoa.

--- SENTENCE 223 ---
An interview survey of local fishermen in 1994-95 showed that all participants believed that fishing for giant clams had declined in living memory, while fewer people believed that fishing for reef fish (70%) or palolo (43%) had declined (Tuilagi & Green 1995).

--- SENTENCE 224 ---
More recently, subsistence fishermen raised concerns that fishing had become increasingly more difficult, while the nightime scuba fishery was operating (Append 1).

--- SENTENCE 225 ---
Some biological studies have also examined the effects of fishing on fisheries resources.

--- SENTENCE 226 ---
Page (1998) demonstrated that parrotfishes were overfished on Tutuila.

--- SENTENCE 227 ---
In contrast, Craig et al (1997) reported that while one of the major target species of surgeonfish (*Acanthurus lineatus*) experienced heavy fishing pressure, it did not appear to be overfished.

--- SENTENCE 228 ---
Giant clams, locally known as *faisua*, are an important food item in Samoa, but their accessibility and life history characteristics make them particularly vulnerable to overharvesting.

--- SENTENCE 229 ---
Green & Craig (1999) examined that status of giant clam populations on eight islands in the Samoan Archipelago, and concluded that they were overfished throughout most of the archipelago.

--- SENTENCE 230 ---
This information was consistent with local fisheries statistics for Tutuila, which showed a decline in the harvest of giant clams over the last two decades (see Green & Craig 1999).

--- SENTENCE 231 ---
One concern is that the remaining individuals are now present in such low densities that their reproductive success, and subsequent recruitment, may be diminished.

--- SENTENCE 232 ---
Green & Craig (1999) demonstrated that Rose Atoll was an important refuge for one of the three species of giant clam (*Tridacna maxima*) that occurs in American Samoa, since it was the only island that still supported a healthy population of those clams.

--- SENTENCE 233 ---
Unfortunately, Rose is not able to act as a refuge for the other clam species also known to occur in Samoa, *Tridacna squamosa*, because it does not occur out there.

--- SENTENCE 234 ---
The presence of subfossil shells also suggests that a third species, *Hippopus hipposus*, used to occur in Samoa, but is now locally extinct (Munro 1986, Nagaoka 1993) except for hatchery reared animals.

--- SENTENCE 235 ---
Whether this is due to overfishing (*H. hippopus* is particularly vulnerable to overfishing because it occurs in shallow water), or a natural reduction in range (Samoa was the eastern extent of its range) is unclear (Munro 1986).

--- SENTENCE 236 ---
In general, the palolo fishery appears to be in relatively good condition on the south side of Tutuila and in Manu'a where most of the fishing for this species occurs (Tuilagi & Green 1995).

--- SENTENCE 237 ---
This is probably because the palolo's coral reef habitat is still in good condition at most locations, and the fishery is very short term (a few days a year) and only targets the reproductive products of the worm (Caspers 1984), so the worms themselves are not harvested.

--- SENTENCE 238 ---
The exception is inner Pago Pago Harbour, where palolo fishing no longer occurs as it did >50 years ago, presumably because of the almost complete destruction of the coral reefs in the area due to dredging, land filling and chronic pollution (Tuilagi & Green 1995).

--- SENTENCE 239 ---
The status of other invertebrate species that are important in the coral fishery (eg octopus, sea urchins and spiny lobsters: Saucerman 1996) is unknown.

--- SENTENCE 240 ---
Destructive fishing practices are illegal in American Samoa, since they can cause severe damage to coral reef habitats (particularly dynamite fishing:

--- SENTENCE 241 ---
Itano 1980, Tuilagi & Green 1995).

--- SENTENCE 242 ---
However, there is some evidence that illegal fishing practices (particularly dynamite fishing, but also the use of traditional fish poisons) continue to be used on Tutuila (Itano 1980, Tutuila and Green 1995, Birkeland et al in prep).

--- SENTENCE 243 ---
For example, in an interview survey of fishermen on Tutuila, 25% of people reported that dynamite fishing had occurred in the last year, while only a few (9%) knew of traditional poisons (*ava niu kini*) being used over the same time period (Tuilagi & Green 1995).

--- SENTENCE 244 ---
In that survey, a higher percentage of people reported the use of these illegal fishing techniques on the north side of the island.

--- SENTENCE 245 ---
This is probably because many of the bays on the north side are relatively remote and unpopulated, and the reefs are not protected by the presence of a village.

--- SENTENCE 246 ---
More recently, some evidence of dynamite fishing has been observed in FBNMS on the south side of the island (Birkeland et al *in prep*).

--- SENTENCE 247 ---
Despite its protected status, Fagatele Bay may be vulnerable to illegal fishing practices, because it is uninhabited and enforcement is intermittent.

--- SENTENCE 248 ---
Furthermore, it is likely that both FBNMS and the isolated reefs in the National Park of American Samoa (NPAS) on Tutuila, were targeted by the nightime scuba fishery (Page 1998, Birkeland et al *in prep*).

--- SENTENCE 249 ---
#### Water Quality

Fortunately, water quality is good around most of American Samoa, because the islands are steep with narrow fringing reefs (and limited lagoon development)

--- SENTENCE 250 ---
so the reefs are continually flushed by clear oceanic waters (Craig 2002).

--- SENTENCE 251 ---
Exceptions include heavy sedimentation at some sites after rain (due to natural causes and poor land use practices), and nutrient enrichment from human and animal waste in populated areas (Craig 2002).

--- SENTENCE 252 ---
This is of particular concern in narrow embayments which are not as well flushed by oceanic water, particularly Pago Pago Harbour, which is considered a Special Management Area.

--- SENTENCE 253 ---
## *Pago Pago Harbour Special Management Area*

Early last century, human habitation in Pago Pago Harbour was restricted to a few small traditional villages, and lush coral reefs lined the shore (Mayor 1924a,b).

--- SENTENCE 254 ---
Since then, the Harbour has experienced some major changes and become a heavily populated urban and industrial area, with a busy port and two tuna canneries (Green et al 1997a).

--- SENTENCE 255 ---
This has resulted in some major changes to the reefs in the Harbour area.

--- SENTENCE 256 ---
In particular, approximately 97% of the reefs in the inner harbour have now been completely destroyed by dredging and filling operations (IUCN/UNEP 1988).

--- SENTENCE 257 ---
There has also been a serious decline in water quality as a result of chemical pollution from industry and agriculture (fuel spills, heavy metals and pesticides) and solid waste disposal (Green et al 1997a).

--- SENTENCE 258 ---
The Harbour also receives high sediment loads after periods of heavy rain, but it is unclear how much of this is natural or has been exacerbated by human activities (since major sediment plumes were reported in the Harbour by Mayor 1924a).

--- SENTENCE 259 ---
Of particular concern has been the chronic eutrophication of the area caused by the effluent from the tuna canneries, which have operated in the inner Harbour since 1956 (Green et al 1997a).

--- SENTENCE 260 ---
Furthermore, a toxicity study in the early 1990s showed that the fish and substrates in the inner Harbour contained high levels of heavy metals and were unfit for human consumption (see Craig 2002).

--- SENTENCE 261 ---
Preliminary results of a toxicity study conducted this year (P. Peshut, ASEPA *pers comm*) indicate that elevated levels of heavy metals (particularly mercury, arsenic and PCBs) continue to be present in fish in the inner Harbour (although lead levels were lower than previously detected).

--- SENTENCE 262 ---
The source of these heavy metals remains unclear, but may involve natural factors (arsenic may be naturally occurring in volcanic soils) or those related to human activities (for mercury, PCBs and lead: P. Peshut *pers comm*).

--- SENTENCE 263 ---
In that last few decades, the reefs in the Harbour have also endured two severe hurricanes (1990 and 1991) and nine fishing vessels grounded during Hurricane Val in 1991 (NOAA 2002b).

--- SENTENCE 264 ---
However, they appear to have escaped the major COTS outbreak in the late 1970s (Green et al 1997a).

--- SENTENCE 265 ---
Several studies have demonstrated that the coral reefs in the Harbour have declined due to poor water quality.

--- SENTENCE 266 ---
For example, a long term study of the "Aua transect" on the reef flat on the east side of Harbour, showed that there had been a serious decline in the coral reef community at that site since it was first surveyed in 1917 (Mayor 1924a, Dahl & Lamberts, 1977, Dahl 1981, Green et al 1997a).

--- SENTENCE 267 ---
For example, the diversity of corals that are particularly vulnerable to poor water quality had declined (eg *Acropora* species: Green et al 1997a).

--- SENTENCE 268 ---
Observations by the Samoan community indicated that the lush coral reefs at Aua disappeared in the 1950s, probably due to a decline in water quality as the result of several human activities that started operating at that time (dredging, tuna canneries, fuel spills:

--- SENTENCE 269 ---
Green et al 1997a).

--- SENTENCE 270 ---
Another long term monitoring program of the coral communities on the other side of the Harbour (in front of the Rainmaker Hotel at Utulei) also showed that the coral communities in the Harbour were declining, presumably from the effects of chronic sedimentation and pollution on coral recruitment (Birkeland et al. 1994, 1996).

--- SENTENCE 271 ---
Mundy (1996) also concluded that the poor condition of the coral communities in the Harbour was probably due to the long term effects of poor water quality.

--- SENTENCE 272 ---
Fortunately, water quality has improved substantially in the Harbour in the last 12 years, since there has been an improvement in the management of waste from the tuna canneries (Green et al 1997a, Craig 2002, ASEPA *unpubl data*).

--- SENTENCE 273 ---
This has resulted in a dramatic reduction in the nutrient levels in the Harbour (Craig 2002, ASEPA *unpubl data*).

--- SENTENCE 274 ---
The shipwrecks were also removed in 1999-2001 (NOAA 2002b), and restoration included removing the vessel structures and debris, and restoring the injured reef flat resources (including transplanting corals to minimise further damage during the cleanup).

--- SENTENCE 275 ---
Unfortunately, fuel spills remain frequent in the Harbour area (P. Peshut *pers comm*).

--- SENTENCE 276 ---
More recent surveys have demonstrated that the coral communities in the outer Harbour may be starting to show some signs of recovery, in response to improved water quality.

--- SENTENCE 277 ---
For example, a survey of the Aua transect in 1999 indicated that the reef flat communities appeared to be in good condition for the first time in decades (healthy coral and crustose coralline algae), which was attributed to improved water quality (Birkeland & Green 1999).

--- SENTENCE 278 ---
There had also been a mass recruitment of *Acropora nana* and *Pocillopora danae*, which resulted in a dramatic increase in living coral cover and abundance on the transect (Birkeland & Green 1999).

--- SENTENCE 279 ---
The fact that *Acropora* species were abundant on the transect again was considered a good indicator of improved water quality, since they are particularly vulnerable to pollution.

--- SENTENCE 280 ---
Similarly, a substantial increase in *Acropora* recruits (particularly *Acropora hyacinthus*) was observed at Utulei in 1999 for the first time in two decades (C. Birkeland *pers comm*), indicating that those reefs may be starting to recover also.

--- SENTENCE 281 ---
Despite these encouraging signs, the coral communities in the Harbour are still a long way from resembling the lush coral communities described by Mayor (1924a,b) early last century.

#### **Long Term Monitoring and Survey Objectives**

Two long term monitoring programs have been underway in American Samoa for some time.

--- SENTENCE 282 ---
Long term monitoring of the "Aua Transect" in Pago Pago Harbour has been in place since 1917 (Mayor 1924a), which makes it the second oldest coral reef monitoring program in the world (Green et al. 1997a).

--- SENTENCE 283 ---
The results of that study provide a valuable long term perspective of how the reefs in the Harbour have changed over the last century (see *Water Quality* above).

--- SENTENCE 284 ---
In contrast, the long term monitoring program of FBNMS has been in place since 1985, although some data are also available for the late 1970s (Green et al 1999).

--- SENTENCE 285 ---
The Sanctuary program provides a valuable opportunity to understand the natural variability and long term trends in coral reefs on Tutuila, in the absence of most anthropogenic processes.

--- SENTENCE 286 ---
While these programs provide valuable information for those sites, they do not provide a broad scale perspective of the condition of the reefs throughout the Territory (although the FBNMS program does include some other sites around Tutuila).

--- SENTENCE 287 ---
In the mid 1990s, a quantitative baseline survey was conducted throughout the Samoan Archipelago to assess the status of the reefs following a series of large scale disturbances (Green 1996a).

--- SENTENCE 288 ---
This detailed survey described the status of the reef fishes, their habitat characteristics (benthic communities at the growth form level), and key macroinvertebrates, in a range of habitat types on eight islands in the Samoan Archipelago (including all five volcanic islands and two remote atolls in American Samoa, and the main island of 'Upolu in independent Samoa).

--- SENTENCE 289 ---
A companion survey of the corals (at the species level) was conducted by Mundy (1996) on the five volcanic islands in American Samoa at the same time.

--- SENTENCE 290 ---
The primary objective of this study is to repeat the baseline survey of the five main volcanic islands of American Samoa (Tutuila, Aunu'u and the Manu'a Islands) conducted by Green (1996a).

--- SENTENCE 291 ---
Unfortunately it was not possible to repeat the survey of 'Upolu and the two remote atolls (Green 1996a), due to logistic constraints.

--- SENTENCE 292 ---
This survey will focus on describing the trends in the coral reef communities on these islands over the last six years.

--- SENTENCE 293 ---
In particular, it will determine if:

- the reefs are recovering from the large scale disturbances of the last few decades; and/or
- there are any detectable impacts from human activities (particularly due to fishing or poor water quality) on these reefs.

--- SENTENCE 294 ---
It will also:

- provide a broad scale perspective for interpreting the results of the long term monitoring programs in FBNMS and Pago Pago Harbour; and
- document two large scale events that took place during the survey (coral bleaching and a mass recruitment event of a major fisheries species).

--- SENTENCE 295 ---
A companion coral survey (at the species level) was conducted at the same time as this survey (using the same transects).

--- SENTENCE 296 ---
The results of that survey are reported separately by Fisk & Birkeland (2002).

--- SENTENCE 297 ---
#### **METHODS**

## *

--- SENTENCE 298 ---
*Description of Study Area**

#### Samoan Archipelago

The Samoan Archipelago is located in the Central Pacific at lat.

--- SENTENCE 299 ---
13-14° S and long.

--- SENTENCE 300 ---
168-172° E, and is divided into two countries: independent Samoa and American Samoa (Fig. 1).

--- SENTENCE 301 ---
Samoa comprises seven islands in the western end of archipelago, including the two large islands of 'Upolu and Savai'i (Fig. 1).

--- SENTENCE 302 ---
American Samoa encompasses five emergent islands of volcanic rock (Tutuila, Aunu'u, and the Manu'a Islands) and two remote atolls (Rose and Swains: Fig. 1).

--- SENTENCE 303 ---
Fig 1 Map of the Samoan Archipelago (map courtesy of NPAS).

!

--- SENTENCE 304 ---
This study will focus on the five main volcanic islands of American Samoa.

--- SENTENCE 305 ---
These islands differ in terms of their size, age, and human habitation.

--- SENTENCE 306 ---
The main island of Tutuila (Fig 1, 2) is the oldest, largest and supports most of the population (97%) and the highest population density (Table 1).

--- SENTENCE 307 ---
The islands of the Manu'a Group (Ofu, Olosega, and Ta'u: Fig. 1, 3), are located 102 km east of Tutuila.

--- SENTENCE 308 ---
These islands are smaller, younger, and have a much lower population density (Table 1).

--- SENTENCE 309 ---
Aunu'u is a small island off the southeast coast of Tutuila (Fig. 2), which has a moderately high population density (Table 1).

--- SENTENCE 310 ---
The population of American Samoa is rapidly increasing.

--- SENTENCE 311 ---
The total population of 57,291 recorded in 2000, represented a 22% increase in the Territory since the last census in 1990 (2.1% per year: Craig 2002).

--- SENTENCE 312 ---
The increase was primarily due to a 24% increase in the population on Tutuila, since the population in Manu'a declined by 20%.

--- SENTENCE 313 ---

#### Reef and Habitat types

Most of the reefs on the volcanic islands of American Samoa are narrow fringing reefs that are close (<200m) to shore.

--- SENTENCE 324 ---
These reefs can be divided into six recognizable habitat types, which differ in their position on the reef profile, depth and degree of wave exposure (described in detail by Green 1996a).

--- SENTENCE 325 ---
At most sites, the reef slope descends from the crest at a slope of 45-90<sup>o</sup> down to the reef base (depth=10- 30m), where it joins the sand flat which stretches away from the reef towards open water

#### Location of Study Sites

The location of each study site is described in Append 2.

--- SENTENCE 326 ---
Geographic co-ordinates (on WGS84 datum) were taken at each site surveyed on the reef slope in 2002 by C. Birkeland and A. Green (Append 2) using a hand held GPS.

--- SENTENCE 327 ---
These co-ordinates were used to plot the location of the study sites on rectified satellite images of the islands (by W. White, DMWR:

--- SENTENCE 328 ---
Figs 2 & 3).

--- SENTENCE 329 ---
However, the co-ordinates recorded for three sites (Aunu'u, Fagaitua, and Lepula) appeared to be incorrect, since they did not represent their correct locations on the images.

--- SENTENCE 330 ---
Therefore, their locations on Figs 2 & 3 are based on site descriptions only (Append 2).

--- SENTENCE 331 ---
New co-ordinates for these sites were taken from the rectified satellite images (by W. White: see Append 2), which will require verification in the next field survey.

--- SENTENCE 332 ---
Where possible, transects started in an easily defined location (eg near a natural landmark such as a channel or *ava*) and were laid in a predefined direction along a depth contour.

--- SENTENCE 333 ---
The location of the transects was described in detail (Append 2) to allow them to be relocated in future surveys.

--- SENTENCE 334 ---
Unfortunately, that was more difficult in Ofu Lagoon.

--- SENTENCE 335 ---
While the starting position could be easily described (Append 2), the actual location of the transects was not well defined because they followed the edges of the coral in the lagoon (and not a depth profile).

--- SENTENCE 336 ---
Therefore, it was possible to lay the transects in slightly different directions in each survey.

--- SENTENCE 337 ---
For that reason, it is recommended that permanent transects be established in the lagoon for future surveys.

--- SENTENCE 338 ---
The two sites surveyed in Ofu Lagoon were in Pools 200 and 400 (Fig 4), which were called Vaoto Lodge and Hurricane House respectively (Append 2).

!

--- SENTENCE 339 ---
[](_page_26_Figure_1.jpeg)

#### **Baseline Survey Design**

A detailed baseline survey of the coral reefs on eight islands in the Samoan Archipelago was conducted from October 1994 to November 1995 (Green 1996a).

--- SENTENCE 340 ---
This survey included all seven islands in American Samoa (five volcanic islands and two remote atolls) and the main island of 'Upolu in Samoa, and provided a rigorous scientific basis for the long term monitoring of these reefs.

--- SENTENCE 341 ---
Key components of the survey included quantitative surveys of benthic communities (at the growth form level), fish communities (at the species level), and key macroinvertebrates (giant clams and COTS).

--- SENTENCE 342 ---
The results are described in detail by Green (1996a) and Green & Craig 1999).

--- SENTENCE 343 ---
All sites surveyed were areas of well developed continuous reef tract.

--- SENTENCE 344 ---
Where possible, sites were distributed around each island to include the variation associated with exposure (Fig 5).

--- SENTENCE 345 ---
Sites on the southern sides of the islands are exposed to the prevailing southeast Trade Winds from April to September.

--- SENTENCE 346 ---
In contrast, sites on the north sides are more protected from the Trade Winds, but tend to be harder hit by hurricanes which occur from October to March.

--- SENTENCE 347 ---
Five of the sites on Tutuila were located within Pago Pago Harbour on the south side of the island, which tends to be relatively protected from the prevailing wind conditions.

--- SENTENCE 348 ---
The number of sites surveyed on each island ranged from 1 to 17 (Append 2, Fig 5), depending on logistic constraints (a combination of the time available on each island, weather conditions, and the area of available reef tract).

--- SENTENCE 349 ---
In the baseline survey, coral reef communities were compared among habitat types on several islands (Green 1996a).

--- SENTENCE 350 ---
Sites were also compared among and within islands based on a single habitat type.

--- SENTENCE 351 ---
Reef slopes (depth=10m) were used for this comparison because they are well represented on each island.

--- SENTENCE 352 ---
It is also the habitat type where fish species richness, density and biomass tend to be highest, which is particularly relevant for measuring the status of coral reef communities and the impacts of human activities (particularly the effects of fishing, much of which takes place on the reef slope).

--- SENTENCE 353 ---
Group Tutuila & Aunu'u Manu'u

--- SENTENCE 354 ---

--- SENTENCE 356 ---
Note: Hurricane House was surveyed for the first time in 2002.

--- SENTENCE 357 ---
Surveys were conducted using five replicate transects at each site using the methods described below for each taxa.

--- SENTENCE 358 ---
#### Reef Fish Communities

Reef fishes were surveyed using visual census techniques along five replicate 50m x 3m transects along the reef slope (depth=10m) at each site (total area=750m² per habitat per site: Green 1996a).

--- SENTENCE 359 ---
These transect dimensions were used because Green (1996d) determined that they yielded the most precise estimate of abundances of highly mobile, diurnal species such as wrasses.

--- SENTENCE 360 ---
Transect lengths were measured using 50m tapes, and transect widths were measured using known body proportions.

--- SENTENCE 361 ---
The size of each fish (total length in cm) was estimated visually and recorded directly onto underwater paper.

--- SENTENCE 362 ---
A restricted family list was used which comprised only those families which are amenable to visual census techniques, because they are relatively large, diurnally active and conspicuous in coloration and behaviour (Table 2).

--- SENTENCE 363 ---
This method excludes species that are not amenable to the technique because they are very small, nocturnal or cryptic in behaviour (eg gobies, blennies, cardinalfish).

--- SENTENCE 364 ---
Fishes were surveyed by three passes along the transect counting different species in each pass.

--- SENTENCE 365 ---
The first count was of large, highly mobile species, which are most likely to be disturbed by the passage of a diver (such as parrotfishes, snappers and emperors).

--- SENTENCE 366 ---
This count was conducted while an assistant followed laying out the tapes, so the observer could concentrate of looking up and ahead on the transect.

--- SENTENCE 367 ---
The tapes then remained *in situ* until all the surveys were completed at that site.

--- SENTENCE 368 ---
The second count was of medium sized mobile families (including most surgeonfishes, butterflyfishes and wrasses), which are less disturbed by the presence of a diver.

--- SENTENCE 369 ---
The third count was of small, site attached species (mostly damselfishes), which are least disturbed by the presence of a diver.

--- SENTENCE 370 ---
Fish counts were be separated by a ~5 minute waiting period.

--- SENTENCE 371 ---
Benthic communities and key macroinvertebrates were surveyed along the same transects after the fish counts were completed (see below), as were the coral communities (see Fisk & Birkeland 2002).

--- SENTENCE 372 ---
Table 2 Reef fish families included in surveys of the Samoan Archipelago (Green 1996a, this survey).

--- SENTENCE 373 ---

|-----------------|--------------------|-------------------------------|
